Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. My wife’s worried about the mortgage and the business, and we’ve been arguing because she feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. I don’t think I’m trying to be careless, but I can see why it looks that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your work situation or anything else lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… overwhelmed maybe a little, yeah, but I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle my job. Yeah… I mean, I do get pulled off track more than I’d like. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.

I’m trying to stay on top of it, but it’s gotten frustrating enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it fidgeting exactly, but sitting still feels kind of useless lately. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Clients have started complaining about missed details and mixed-up scheduling, and my apprentices don’t trust my direction the way they used to. At home, the mortgage and the business are turning into constant arguments because my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.

But it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.

At home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

At home, the mortgage and the business are turning into constant arguments because my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.

But it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. Clients have started complaining about little details and scheduling getting mixed up, and then I bring that stress home. My apprentices don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to either, which… honestly bothers me more than I’d like to admit.

And at home, the mortgage and the business stuff keep turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. So even when we’re spending time together, it doesn’t always feel relaxed. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.

And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.\n\nBut it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. Clients have started complaining about little details and scheduling getting mixed up, and then I bring that stress home. My apprentices don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to either, which… honestly bothers me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and the business stuff keep turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. So even when we’re spending time together, it doesn’t always feel relaxed. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Clients have started complaining about missed details and mixed-up scheduling, and my apprentices don’t trust my direction the way they used to. At home, the mortgage and the business are turning into constant arguments because my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, at least to a noticeable degree. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function at all, because I am still working and trying to keep things moving.

But clients have started complaining about details I’ve missed and scheduling getting mixed up, and that’s not like me. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.

I’m trying to stay on top of it, but it’s gotten frustrating enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it fidgeting exactly, but sitting still feels kind of useless lately. I don’t mean to be. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nEven when I’m not actively on a job, if I’m trying to sit down with an estimate or a code form, something small will pull me away — a tool noise, a text coming in, or I’ll suddenly get this idea about the workshop and think, “Oh, I should write that down,” and then I’m off on that instead.\n\nAnd then I come back and realize I mixed something up or didn’t finish the first thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really sadness, no… I wouldn’t call it depression. If anything, it’s kind of the opposite sometimes, which I know maybe sounds better than it is.

I get into these really pumped-up moods, like I’m talking about the shop like we’re about to become the main solar outfit in town. I’ll feel pretty sure of the plan, excited about it. But then if my wife questions it, or one of the apprentices pushes back even a little, I can get… sharper than I mean to. Irritated over things that probably don’t deserve that reaction.

I don’t like admitting that, because I’m not trying to be difficult. But yeah, my mood has been more up and more easily irritated than sad, if that makes sense.

with them over nothing.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I’ve been going nonstop from commercial jobs and train a whole crew right now, even though part of me knows my licensing and experience aren’t quite where they need to be for all that.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it happens in both places, though work is where it’s easiest to see.

I mean, I can still get things done, but what finally made me think I should come in is I can’t seem to stay with one estimate or code form the way I used to. A tool noise, a text coming in, or even some new idea about the workshop will pull me off, and then I realize I’ve jumped to something else and the jobs start getting mixed up.

At home it’s similar, just less obvious maybe. My mind keeps drifting back to the shop or the next thing I should be doing. I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it’s not just one place. Does that make sense?

I don’t mean to be. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nEven when I’m not actively on a job, if I’m trying to sit down with an estimate or a code form, something small will pull me away — a tool noise, a text coming in, or I’ll suddenly get this idea about the workshop and think, “Oh, I should write that down,” and then I’m off on that instead.\n\nAnd then I come back and realize I mixed something up or didn’t finish the first thing. That’s kind of what finally made me think, okay, maybe this is becoming a problem. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these difficulties with focus and restlessness as something that has been present for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been building over the last… about seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept thinking it was just the pressure of that, which maybe it is partly, but it hasn’t really felt like a lifelong thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing problems in your relationships with your wife or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say it’s not, but it is starting to.\n\nAt work, clients have complained a couple times about details I missed or schedules getting mixed up, and that’s embarrassing. My apprentices have noticed too, I think — they don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I argue more than we used to. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.\n\nBut it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.\n\nBut it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and business stuff keeps turning into arguments with my wife. Is that what you mean by significantly?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness or depression, alongside these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not really sadness, no… I wouldn’t call it depression. If anything, it’s kind of the opposite sometimes, which I know maybe sounds better than it is.\n\nI get into these really pumped-up moods, like I’m talking about the shop like we’re about to become the main solar outfit in town. Irritated over things that probably don’t deserve that reaction.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because I’m not trying to be difficult. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nEven when I’m not actively on a job, if I’m trying to sit down with an estimate or a code form, something small will pull me away — a tool noise, a text coming in, or I’ll suddenly get this idea about the workshop and think, “Oh, I should write that down,” and then I’m off on that instead.\n\nAnd then I come back and realize I mixed something up or didn’t finish the first thing. That’s kind of what finally made me think, okay, maybe this is becoming a problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused and sitting still, or is this something that's developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve had normal restlessness like anyone, but this feels more recent.\n\nIt’s probably been building for… maybe seven months? Around when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept telling myself it was just the stress of that, which maybe it partly is, but yeah, that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these difficulties with focus and restlessness as something that has been present for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been building over the last… about seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept thinking it was just the pressure of that, which maybe it is partly, but it hasn’t really felt like a lifelong thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing problems in your relationships with your wife or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say it’s not, but it is starting to.\n\nAt work, clients have complained a couple times about details I missed or schedules getting mixed up, and that’s embarrassing. My apprentices have noticed too, I think — they don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I argue more than we used to. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.\n\nBut it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, at least to a noticeable degree. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function at all, because I am still working and trying to keep things moving.\n\nBut clients have started complaining about details I’ve missed and scheduling getting mixed up, and that’s not like me. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and business stuff keeps turning into arguments with my wife. A tool noise, a text coming in, or even some new idea about the workshop will pull me off, and then I realize I’ve jumped to something else and the jobs start getting mixed up.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less obvious maybe. My mind keeps drifting back to the shop or the next thing I should be doing. I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it’s not just one place. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness during leisure activities or when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s tied to work, or at least that’s where I notice it the most. I don’t want to overstate it like I can’t sit still for anything.\n\nBut even when I’m not supposed to be working, my head kind of gets pulled back there. What really got me to come in was that I can’t keep my attention on one estimate or one code form the way I used to. A tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop can pull me off track, and then I realize the jobs are getting mixed up.\n\nSo leisure time… I guess I don’t separate from it very well. But the clearest problem is still with those work tasks, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember being told as a kid that I was unusually hyper or anything like that.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as what’s been happening lately. But the clearest problem is still with those work tasks, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember being told as a kid that I was unusually hyper or anything like that.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as what’s been happening lately. I don’t want to give you a wrong answer just to make it fit. Maybe my parents would remember better, but from what I can say, I don’t have a clear childhood history of that. Does that help?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness have been present in some form since you were a child, even if they weren't as noticeable or problematic back then?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can honestly say it goes back to childhood, no. At least, not in a way I clearly remember.

This feels more like something that’s been building over the last several months… maybe about seven months now, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I started noticing it more. Is that the kind of timeline you mean?

I’m trying to stay on top of it, but it’s gotten frustrating enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it fidgeting exactly, but sitting still feels kind of useless lately. My wife’s worried about the mortgage and the business, and we’ve been arguing because she feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. I don’t think I’m trying to be careless, but I can see why it looks that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your work situation or anything else lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… overwhelmed maybe a little, yeah, but I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle my job. I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything wild, at least I don’t think so. Sometimes I make quick calls because it feels like the right thing in the moment, especially with work stuff, but I usually have a reason for it.\n\nWhat do you mean by impulse exactly—like spending money or picking fights, or just making decisions fast?\n\nDoctor: Have you been more argumentative or short-tempered with family or friends lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been a little sharper than usual. I don’t mean to be. It’s not like I’m trying to pick fights or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll get really fired up talking about where the shop could go, like we could really become the main solar outfit around here, and I’m kind of energized about it. But then if my wife or one of the apprentices pushes back or questions the plan, I can… snap a bit. Get short with them over stuff that probably isn’t that big a deal.\n\nI know that doesn’t sound great. I’m working on catching myself, but yeah, I think they’d probably say I’ve been more argumentative.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do get pulled off track more than I’d like. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nEven when I’m not actively on a job, if I’m trying to sit down with an estimate or a code form, something small will pull me away — a tool noise, a text coming in, or I’ll suddenly get this idea about the workshop and think, “Oh, I should write that down,” and then I’m off on that instead.\n\nAnd then I come back and realize I mixed something up or didn’t finish the first thing. That’s kind of what finally made me think, okay, maybe this is becoming a problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused and sitting still, or is this something that's developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve had normal restlessness like anyone, but this feels more recent.\n\nIt’s probably been building for… maybe seven months? Around when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept telling myself it was just the stress of that, which maybe it partly is, but yeah, that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these difficulties with focus and restlessness as something that has been present for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been building over the last… about seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept thinking it was just the pressure of that, which maybe it is partly, but it hasn’t really felt like a lifelong thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing problems in your relationships with your wife or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say it’s not, but it is starting to.\n\nAt work, clients have complained a couple times about details I missed or schedules getting mixed up, and that’s embarrassing. My apprentices have noticed too, I think — they don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I argue more than we used to. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.\n\nBut it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.\n\nBut it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. Clients have started complaining about little details and scheduling getting mixed up, and then I bring that stress home. My apprentices don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to either, which… honestly bothers me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and the business stuff keep turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. So even when we’re spending time together, it doesn’t always feel relaxed. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, at least to a noticeable degree. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function at all, because I am still working and trying to keep things moving.\n\nBut clients have started complaining about details I’ve missed and scheduling getting mixed up, and that’s not like me. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and business stuff keeps turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. So yeah, I guess it is interfering with daily life more than I’d like. Is that what you mean by significantly?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness or depression, alongside these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not really sadness, no… I wouldn’t call it depression. If anything, it’s kind of the opposite sometimes, which I know maybe sounds better than it is.\n\nI get into these really pumped-up moods, like I’m talking about the shop like we’re about to become the main solar outfit in town. I’ll feel pretty sure of the plan, excited about it. But then if my wife questions it, or one of the apprentices pushes back even a little, I can get… sharper than I mean to. A tool noise, a text coming in, or even some new idea about the workshop will pull me off, and then I realize I’ve jumped to something else and the jobs start getting mixed up.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less obvious maybe. I’m trying to stay on top of it, but it’s gotten frustrating enough that I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it fidgeting exactly, but sitting still feels kind of useless lately. I’ve been keeping myself moving—job sites, then bids, then back in the workshop rewiring test panels or starting some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one. It’s productive, sort of, but… yeah, I guess I do feel pretty restless when I’m not doing something. My wife’s worried about the mortgage and the business, and we’ve been arguing because she feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. I don’t think I’m trying to be careless, but I can see why it looks that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your work situation or anything else lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… overwhelmed maybe a little, yeah, but I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle my job. I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything wild, at least I don’t think so. Sometimes I make quick calls because it feels like the right thing in the moment, especially with work stuff, but I usually have a reason for it.\n\nWhat do you mean by impulse exactly—like spending money or picking fights, or just making decisions fast?\n\nDoctor: Have you been more argumentative or short-tempered with family or friends lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been a little sharper than usual. I don’t mean to be. It’s not like I’m trying to pick fights or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll get really fired up talking about where the shop could go, like we could really become the main solar outfit around here, and I’m kind of energized about it. But then if my wife or one of the apprentices pushes back or questions the plan, I can… snap a bit. Get short with them over stuff that probably isn’t that big a deal.\n\nI know that doesn’t sound great. I’m working on catching myself, but yeah, I think they’d probably say I’ve been more argumentative.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do get pulled off track more than I’d like. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely scattered, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nEven when I’m not actively on a job, if I’m trying to sit down with an estimate or a code form, something small will pull me away — a tool noise, a text coming in, or I’ll suddenly get this idea about the workshop and think, “Oh, I should write that down,” and then I’m off on that instead.\n\nAnd then I come back and realize I mixed something up or didn’t finish the first thing. That’s kind of what finally made me think, okay, maybe this is becoming a problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused and sitting still, or is this something that's developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these difficulties with focus and restlessness as something that has been present for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been building over the last… about seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept thinking it was just the pressure of that, which maybe it is partly, but it hasn’t really felt like a lifelong thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing problems in your relationships with your wife or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say it’s not, but it is starting to.\n\nAt work, clients have complained a couple times about details I missed or schedules getting mixed up, and that’s embarrassing. My apprentices have noticed too, I think — they don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I argue more than we used to. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.\n\nBut it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.\n\nBut it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. Clients have started complaining about little details and scheduling getting mixed up, and then I bring that stress home. My apprentices don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to either, which… honestly bothers me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and the business stuff keep turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. So even when we’re spending time together, it doesn’t always feel relaxed. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, at least to a noticeable degree. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function at all, because I am still working and trying to keep things moving.\n\nBut clients have started complaining about details I’ve missed and scheduling getting mixed up, and that’s not like me. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and business stuff keeps turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. So yeah, I guess it is interfering with daily life more than I’d like. Is that what you mean by significantly?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness or depression, alongside these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not really sadness, no… I wouldn’t call it depression. If anything, it’s kind of the opposite sometimes, which I know maybe sounds better than it is.\n\nI get into these really pumped-up moods, like I’m talking about the shop like we’re about to become the main solar outfit in town. I’ll feel pretty sure of the plan, excited about it. But then if my wife questions it, or one of the apprentices pushes back even a little, I can get… sharper than I mean to. Irritated over things that probably don’t deserve that reaction.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because I’m not trying to be difficult. But yeah, my mood has been more up and more easily irritated than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble staying focused and feeling restless both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it happens in both places, though work is where it’s easiest to see.\n\nI mean, I can still get things done, but what finally made me think I should come in is I can’t seem to stay with one estimate or code form the way I used to. A tool noise, a text coming in, or even some new idea about the workshop will pull me off, and then I realize I’ve jumped to something else and the jobs start getting mixed up.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less obvious maybe. I mean, I’ve had normal restlessness like anyone, but this feels more recent.\n\nIt’s probably been building for… maybe seven months? Around when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept telling myself it was just the stress of that, which maybe it partly is, but yeah, that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that sound like the kind of timeline you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these difficulties with focus and restlessness as something that has been present for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been building over the last… about seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. I kept thinking it was just the pressure of that, which maybe it is partly, but it hasn’t really felt like a lifelong thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing problems in your relationships with your wife or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say it’s not, but it is starting to.\n\nAt work, clients have complained a couple times about details I missed or schedules getting mixed up, and that’s embarrassing. My apprentices have noticed too, I think — they don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I argue more than we used to. Mostly about the mortgage and the business stuff. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk, and I can see why she’d feel that way, even if I’m trying not to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but yeah, it’s causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset would you say these difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do, mostly.\n\nBut it does bother me. When clients complain about missed details or scheduling getting mixed up, I take that pretty hard. And with my apprentices, feeling like they don’t quite trust my direction the way they used to… that’s upsetting.\n\nAt home it’s probably worse emotionally. The mortgage and the business keep turning into arguments, and my wife feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. I don’t like that at all. So yeah, it’s causing me distress — not completely overwhelming, but definitely more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends because of your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t think it’s that I don’t want to be around my family or anything like that. I still care about all of that.\n\nBut it’s harder to enjoy it when my head is on the business, or I’m worrying I missed something. Clients have started complaining about little details and scheduling getting mixed up, and then I bring that stress home. My apprentices don’t seem to trust my direction the way they used to either, which… honestly bothers me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and the business stuff keep turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting what we built at risk. So even when we’re spending time together, it doesn’t always feel relaxed. I don’t know if that answers it right, but yes, it’s getting in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are significantly interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, at least to a noticeable degree. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function at all, because I am still working and trying to keep things moving.\n\nBut clients have started complaining about details I’ve missed and scheduling getting mixed up, and that’s not like me. My apprentices seem like they don’t trust my direction the way they used to, which is… hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, the mortgage and business stuff keeps turning into arguments with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk. So yeah, I guess it is interfering with daily life more than I’d like. Is that what you mean by significantly?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness or depression, alongside these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not really sadness, no… I wouldn’t call it depression. If anything, it’s kind of the opposite sometimes, which I know maybe sounds better than it is.\n\nI get into these really pumped-up moods, like I’m talking about the shop like we’re about to become the main solar outfit in town. I’ll feel pretty sure of the plan, excited about it. But then if my wife questions it, or one of the apprentices pushes back even a little, I can get… sharper than I mean to. Irritated over things that probably don’t deserve that reaction.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because I’m not trying to be difficult. But yeah, my mood has been more up and more easily irritated than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble staying focused and feeling restless both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it happens in both places, though work is where it’s easiest to see.\n\nI mean, I can still get things done, but what finally made me think I should come in is I can’t seem to stay with one estimate or code form the way I used to. A tool noise, a text coming in, or even some new idea about the workshop will pull me off, and then I realize I’ve jumped to something else and the jobs start getting mixed up.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less obvious maybe. My mind keeps drifting back to the shop or the next thing I should be doing. I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it’s not just one place. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness during leisure activities or when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s tied to work, or at least that’s where I notice it the most. I don’t want to overstate it like I can’t sit still for anything.\n\nBut even when I’m not supposed to be working, my head kind of gets pulled back there. What really got me to come in was that I can’t keep my attention on one estimate or one code form the way I used to. A tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop can pull me off track, and then I realize the jobs are getting mixed up.\n\nSo leisure time… I guess I don’t separate from it very well. But the clearest problem is still with those work tasks, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember being told as a kid that I was unusually hyper or anything like that.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as what’s been happening lately. I don’t want to give you a wrong answer just to make it fit. Maybe my parents would remember better, but from what I can say, I don’t have a clear childhood history of that. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness have been present in some form since you were a child, even if they weren't as noticeable or problematic back then?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can honestly say it goes back to childhood, no. At least, not in a way I clearly remember.\n\nThis feels more like something that’s been building over the last several months… maybe about seven months now, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I started noticing it more. Is that the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness have been a consistent part of your behavior over the last 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.
